## 🤖 Claude *(04:17:33)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

The MONTAR addendum has good structural elements like asymmetry and depth planes, but it's missing explicit shot framing and foreground occlusion as intentional design choices. Looking at the actual data, foreground occlusion does appear in about 15% of frames, though it seems to happen incidentally from the stack structure rather than by deliberate direction—but that's still a meaningful presence.

Now I'm realizing the key distinction: the occluder is never formally declared in the grammar, so when it appears it's a byproduct of the layering system. More importantly, all the planes in the stack sit on the far side of the camera (z ≤ -1635), meaning there's no plane positioned in front of the camera-facing content. True foreground occlusion in compositional terms would be an element closer to the camera than everything else, deliberately cropped by the frame edge and heavily blurred, taking up significant screen space. Let me measure whether the nearest-to-camera elements actually occupy that kind of frame presence and whether they're being cut off by the edges.

I'm going to nail down a cleaner measurement: checking if any layer's projected bounding box extends beyond the 1280×720 viewport while also being blurred by 3 pixels or more—that would be the signature of foreground occlusion. Or even simpler: what's the maximum blur ever applied to layers positioned in front of the focus plane in each scene? If those front layers never exceed 2 pixels of blur, there's no foreground bokeh effect at all.

Now I'm identifying the core lens-related issues: the depth-of-field effect doesn't align with any camera parameters (an 8.43× discrepancy), the lighting is hardcoded with no directional variation despite paper-cut style relying on variable contact shadows for depth, there's no shot-size specification anywhere in the spec despite perceived scale being a critical visual factor, and the hero elements are all identical width with no variation across the scene.

The two EP07 scenes are continuous takes without any cuts, the camera only translates rather than rotating, and there's no texture or grain in the render itself—only dithering applied in post-processing.

Now I need to verify the metronome timing at 1.18 seconds in the scene structure file, then double-check the grain implementation and camera move counts to finalize which findings to include.

</details>

## 🤖 Claude *(04:18:16)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

The foreground blur does hit its limit, so I should drop the "no foreground occlusion" finding as a standalone point—it's not as significant as I initially thought. Now I'm looking at the grain settings, where the default value gets converted to a noise filter, and I'm checking what grade.ts contains.

The grade.ts file isn't referenced anywhere in the EP07 render pipeline, and the actual ffmpeg command for rendering has no video filters applied at all—meaning the final scenes have zero grain, zero grade, and zero texture applied. The only texture-like element is a fixed CSS inset shadow that's identical across every scene. So finding #5 holds up: the rendered pixels are completely clean with no paper fiber, grain, halation, or vignette variation.

Now I'm counting which camera moves are actually expressible in the engine—it only executes translate3d transforms, and while the cinematic grammar defines ten camera move types, I need to figure out which ones can actually be implemented. The lockedOff move should work with flat keyframes, but there's always a drift component applied by default, though I can override that by setting the drift value to zero.

Checking the other moves: dollyIn and dollyOut work via Z keyframes, but lateral truck movement and crane rises are impossible because X and Y are driven only by a sine wave drift with no keyframe control. Orbit, whipPan, and handheld moves all require camera rotation which the engine doesn't support. RackFocus is achievable through the focus stations system, and push is just dollyIn by another name. So out of the ten defined moves, only a handful are actually authorable. The agent's own table confirms this: only the Z-axis and drift are keyframed. So that's exactly **1 of 6 degrees of freedom**. I also notice that `perspective-origin` is defined in the spec as a camera property but gets baked into CSS at build time, making it a static constant per scene. Since animating perspective-origin is essentially pan/tilt in CSS 3D, the straightforward fix would be to make it a runtime keyframed property instead of a build-time constant.

Let me verify that line 254 is indeed build-time and check the hardcoded focus pull speed.

Checking whether the cinematic grammar definitions are actually reachable from the MONTAR rail system — they might be isolated from the runtime entirely, which would explain why they're not being used.

</details>

## 🤖 Claude *(04:19:06)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

I've found the smoking gun: every shadow in the engine has zero horizontal offset, meaning the light source is always directly overhead regardless of the scene or layer. The Y-offsets are hardcoded by type rather than calculated from the Z gap or derived from any specification.

Every cutout also gets the same cyan rim glow applied universally, which is exactly the anti-pattern that Higgsfield explicitly warns against—a static edge glow that prevents the engine from reacting to actual lighting. The A43 specification, which is the single source of truth for the MONTAR language, mentions light and shadow only twice and neither time as an actual rule; one reference notes that photorealistic lighting is what the competing engine does well, and the other is just a performance measurement. So the foundational spec has zero light doctrine.

The cost of fixing this is provably negligible—the spec itself says all blur, glow, shadow, and nebula effects combined cost only about 30 milliseconds per frame, well within noise. So I can close this gap without any measurable performance penalty. That's the decisive combination: the gap is real and closing it is free.

I'm now cataloging five strong, proven gaps in the system, starting with depth-of-field ignoring camera distance and moving into the light source problem.

The remaining gaps are shot size—there are zero tokens for it across thousands of lines, so perceived width is just an undeclared side effect with eight identical heroes all at the same width on a monotone ramp. Camera control is also severely limited, using only three degrees of freedom with hardwired sine motion on the horizontal axes and only the z-axis keyframed, plus a baked perspective origin that could be freed up to handle pan and tilt for no cost. Every focus pull across every scene is locked to exactly 0.8 seconds, consuming between a third and half of each scene in transition. And there's a sixth gap emerging around texture handling. I'm deciding whether to consolidate these observations or keep them separate. The cinematic grammar piece could fold into shot size since that's where the SHOTS logic lives, but it's really more about governance than visual language. The fixed pull timing is distinct from the depth-of-field blur function itself—both owned by the DoP but addressing different concerns—so I'll keep that as its own point. I should also verify whether the white sticker stroke, which the Sovereign approved as the channel signature, gets applied consistently across the engine. width varies across the layers because each source PNG has a different resolution, so the stroke gets baked in at different pixel densities. I need to measure the actual PNG dimensions to confirm this.

</details>

## 🤖 Claude *(04:19:28)*

<details>
<summary>💭 Thinking Process</summary>

Most assets are consistently sized at 2048×2048, though microfone-v4 is an outlier at 1024—but that's not relevant since it's not used in estrutura-pilha. The real problem is that strokes are baked at a fixed pixel size on the 2048px canvas, then displayed at 505 CSS pixels and scaled by a factor ranging from 0.37 to 1.11, which means the same object's stroke width varies significantly depending on the scaling applied.

Actually, that's correct perspective physics—the stroke should scale as objects move closer or farther. And across different layers at the same moment, stroke widths differ by up to 2.2× due to their different Z positions, which is also correct perspective. Since the stroke is a 2D graphic device like a sticker outline rather than a physical object, this behavior matches real paper-cutout physics where the white border represents the physical paper margin. So there's no issue here.

Now I need to verify something critical I haven't checked yet: whether the engine applies blur to the background consistently and whether the vignette stays static. I already know the vignette is static, but I should also check if there's any lint or assertion that validates projected size, since the documentation mentions the assert remains pure without browser dependencies.

At t=9, the blur ladder produces a perfectly linear sequence—0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 10—stepping in exact 1px increments because the focus parameter equals the plane spacing at 95. This creates blur values that are mathematically precise integers, revealing the mechanical signature of the system, whereas real optics would show a hyperbolic and asymmetric curve instead.

The entire scene is built on arithmetic progressions: time increments of 1.18 seconds, depth spacing of 95 pixels, and blur changing by exactly 1 pixel per plane. This is why the composition reads as algorithmically generated—it literally is, with the loop index driving all three dimensions simultaneously. I'm identifying the key lacunae to explain this system: the depth-of-field behavior that ignores camera distance, the arithmetic ladder structure across time and depth, and the lighting setup with its 12 shadows and cyan rim treatment but no traditional key lights.
